4.4.3
Dynamic Panel Data Models
Many financial relationships are inherently dynamic, with lagged dependent variables
capturing persistence, partial adjustment, or momentum. The dynamic panel model is:
yit = ρyi,t−1 + xT
itβ + αi + ϵit.
(73)
The inclusion of yi,t−1 renders the FE estimator inconsistent for fixed T as N →∞(Nickell
bias) (Nickell, 1981). The magnitude of the bias is O(1/T) and can be substantial for
short panels (e.g., T ≤10).
The Arellano-Bond GMM estimator (Arellano and Bond, 1991) addresses this
endogeneity by first-differencing to eliminate αi:
∆yit = ρ∆yi,t−1 + ∆xT
itβ + ∆ϵit,
(74)
and instrumenting the endogenous ∆yi,t−1 with lagged levels yi,t−2, yi,t−3, . . . under the
assumption of no serial correlation in ϵit. The Blundell-Bond system GMM estimator (Blundell and Bond, 1998) augments the moment conditions with equations in levels
instrumented by lagged differences, substantially improving efficiency, especially when
ρ ≈1.
In empirical finance, dynamic panel methods are essential for studying capital structure adjustment speeds (Flannery and Rangan, 2006), dividend smoothing (Lintner,
1956), and performance persistence in mutual funds (Carhart, 1997).
4.5
Instrumental Variables (IV) and Endogeneity
Endogeneity—the correlation between regressors and the error term—represents a fundamental threat to causal inference in observational financial data. Sources of endogeneity
include:
• Omitted Variables: Unobserved firm quality, managerial ability, or risk preferences simultaneously affect both the treatment (e.g., leverage) and outcome (e.g.,
profitability).
• Measurement Error: Classical errors-in-variables bias coefficients toward zero;
non-classical measurement error has unpredictable effects.
• Simultaneity/Reverse Causality: Mutual determination of variables, such as
the relationship between market liquidity and volatility, or corporate governance
and firm value.
Instrumental Variables (IV) estimation provides a strategy for consistent estimation
under endogeneity. An instrument zi for endogenous regressor xi must satisfy two conditions:
1. Relevance (First Stage): Cov(zi, xi)̸ = 0. The instrument must have explanatory power for the endogenous variable.
2. Exogeneity (Exclusion Restriction): Cov(zi, ϵi) = 0. The instrument affects
the outcome only through its effect on the endogenous regressor.
44

The Two-Stage Least Squares (2SLS) estimator is the most common IV estimator:
Stage 1:
ˆxi = zT
i (ZTZ)−1ZTX,
(75)
Stage 2:
ˆβ2SLS = ( ˆXT ˆX)−1 ˆXTy = (XTPZX)−1XTPZy,
(76)
where PZ = Z(ZTZ)−1ZT is the projection matrix onto the instrument space. The 2SLS
estimator is consistent and asymptotically normal under the IV assumptions, but its
finite-sample properties depend critically on instrument strength.
Weak Instruments and Robust Inference
Weak instruments—instruments with
low correlation with the endogenous regressor—exacerbate finite-sample bias and distort
inference. The first-stage F-statistic for the exclusion of instruments from the first-stage
regression serves as a diagnostic: F > 10 is a conventional threshold for adequate instrument strength (Staiger and Stock, 1997). However, Lee and Porter (2022) demonstrate
that F > 104.7 is required for 5% worst-case rejection probability in overidentified models.
When instruments are weak, standard t-tests based on 2SLS have distorted size. Robust alternatives include:
• Anderson-Rubin (AR) Test: Tests the null H0 : β = β0 directly using the
reduced form, remaining valid regardless of instrument strength.
• Limited Information Maximum Likelihood (LIML): Minimizes the ratio of
minimized residual variances under the structural and reduced forms.
LIML is
median-unbiased and exhibits less finite-sample dispersion than 2SLS under weak
instruments (Anderson and Rubin, 1949).
• Continuously Updated GMM (CUE): Employs an objective function that is
invariant to normalization and has better finite-sample properties (Hansen, 1996).
IV Applications in Empirical Finance
Instrumental variables strategies have been
creatively deployed to address endogeneity in various financial contexts:
• Corporate Governance and Firm Value: Bennedsen and Wolfenzon (2003) use
founder succession as an instrument for family control.
• Bank Lending and Real Effects: Khwaja and Mian (2008) exploit exogenous
variation in liquidity shocks across Pakistani banks.
• Geographic Instruments: Bartik (1991) shift-share (Bartik) instruments exploit
local industry composition interacted with national shocks.
• Regulatory Discontinuities: use loan size cutoffs in credit registries to identify
covenant violations.
4.6
Robust Regression
Financial data are notoriously prone to outliers and heavy-tailed error distributions that
violate the normality assumption underlying OLS. Robust regression methods limit the
influence of anomalous observations, providing reliable estimates without requiring explicit outlier identification.
45

Quantile Regression
While OLS models the conditional mean E[Y |X], Koenker and
Bassett (1978) quantile regression models the conditional quantiles Qτ(Y |X) = inf{y :
FY |X(y) ≥τ}, providing a comprehensive characterization of the conditional distribution.
The τ-th quantile regression estimator minimizes the asymmetric absolute loss function:
ˆβτ = arg min
β
n
X
i=1
ρτ(yi −xT
i β),
(80)
where ρτ(u) = u(τ −1u<0) is the check (tick) function. Setting τ = 0.05 directly estimates the 5% Value-at-Risk (VaR), making quantile regression a natural tool for tail risk
modeling (Engle and Manganelli, 2004).
Quantile regression offers several advantages:
• Robustness to Outliers: The influence function is bounded for the median (τ =
0.5), providing resistance to y-outliers (though not to leverage points).
• Heterogeneous Effects: Coefficients βτ can vary across quantiles, revealing whether
predictors affect the center, tails, or entire distribution differently.
• Distribution-Free: No distributional assumptions are imposed on the errors.
Least Absolute Deviation (LAD) and Median Regression
The special case τ =
0.5 yields Least Absolute Deviation (LAD) or median regression, which is the MLE for
Laplace errors and highly robust to outliers. LAD estimates can be computed efficiently
via linear programming (Koenker, 2005).
Beyond Mean and Median: Expectile Regression
Expectiles (Newey and Powell,
1987) generalize quantiles to an asymmetric least squares framework. The τ-th expectile
minimizes E[ρLS
τ (Y −µ)] with ρLS
τ (u) = |τ −1u<0|u2. Expectile regression is more sensitive
to tail events than quantile regression and is elicitable, making it attractive for Expected
Shortfall estimation (Ziegel, 2016).
46

5
Financial Time Series Analysis
Time series analysis constitutes the statistical backbone of quantitative finance, providing
the essential machinery for modeling the temporal dynamics of asset returns, volatilities,
and dependencies. Unlike cross-sectional data, financial time series are characterized by
a constellation of stylized facts that defy the assumptions of classical statistical methods:
serial dependence in returns and squared returns, volatility clustering, leverage effects,
long memory, non-stationarity, and the presence of jumps and microstructure noise at
high frequencies. This section provides a comprehensive treatment of univariate and multivariate time series methodologies, progressing from foundational concepts of stationarity
and linear models through nonlinear volatility dynamics to the specialized econometrics
of high-frequency data.
5.1
Stationarity and Unit Root Processes
The concept of stationarity is fundamental to time series analysis, as it underpins the
validity of most estimation and inference procedures. A stationary process possesses timeinvariant probabilistic structure, ensuring that sample moments converge to population
counterparts and that forecasting is feasible.
5.1.1
Definitions and Taxonomy of Stationarity
Definition 5.1 (Strict (Strong) Stationarity). A stochastic process {Yt}t∈is strictly
stationary if for any finite collection of time indices t1, t2, . . . , tk ∈and any lag h ∈,
the joint distribution of (Yt1, Yt2, . . . , Ytk) is identical to that of (Yt1+h, Yt2+h, . . . , Ytk+h).
Equivalently, the finite-dimensional distributions are shift-invariant.
Strict stationarity is a strong condition that is difficult to verify empirically and
imposes no restrictions on the existence of moments. For practical econometric work,
the weaker concept of covariance stationarity is typically sufficient.
Definition 5.2 (Weak (Covariance) Stationarity). A process {Yt}t∈is weakly stationary or covariance stationary if:
1. E[Yt] = µ < ∞for all t (constant mean),
2. Var(Yt) = γ0 < ∞for all t (finite, constant variance),
3. Cov(Yt, Yt−h) = γh for all t and h (autocovariance depends only on lag h).
Under Gaussianity, weak stationarity implies strict stationarity. The autocorrelation
function (ACF) is defined as ρh = γh/γ0 for h = 0, ±1, ±2, . . ., with ρ0 = 1 and ρh = ρ−h.
Ergodicity
Stationarity alone does not guarantee that sample moments converge to
population moments. A stationary process is ergodic (for the mean) if
1
T
PT
t=1 Yt
a.s.
−−→
E[Yt] as T →∞.
Ergodicity requires that the dependence between observations decays sufficiently rapidly; essentially, the process must asymptotically forget its initial
conditions.
Gaussian stationary processes with absolutely summable autocovariances
(P∞
h=0 |γh| < ∞) are ergodic.
47

5.1.2
Unit Root Testing and Non-Stationarity
Many financial time series, particularly asset prices, exchange rates, and macroeconomic
fundamentals, exhibit non-stationarity in levels. The canonical model for such series
is the random walk with drift:
Yt = µ + Yt−1 + ϵt,
ϵt ∼WN(0, σ2).
(81)
First-differencing yields a stationary series: ∆Yt = Yt −Yt−1 = µ+ϵt, which is stationary.
A series that becomes stationary after d differences is said to be integrated of order d,
denoted Yt ∼I(d). Most financial price series are I(1), while returns are I(0).
The Augmented Dickey-Fuller (ADF) Test
Dickey and Fuller (1979) and Dickey
and Fuller (1981) developed the ADF test for the null hypothesis of a unit root. The test
is based on the auxiliary regression:
∆Yt = α + βt + γYt−1 +
p
X
i=1
δi∆Yt−i + ϵt,
(82)
where the lagged difference terms ∆Yt−i are included to whiten the residuals and account
for serial correlation. The null hypothesis is H0 : γ = 0 (unit root, Yt ∼I(1)) against the
one-sided alternative H1 : γ < 0 (stationarity, Yt ∼I(0)). The test statistic is the t-ratio
for ˆγ:
ADFτ =
ˆγ
SE(ˆγ).
(83)
Crucially, under H0, the asymptotic distribution of ADFτ is non-standard and depends on the deterministic components included in the regression (none, intercept only,
or intercept and trend).
Critical values, tabulated by Dickey and Fuller (1981) and
refined by MacKinnon (1996), are more negative than standard normal critical values.
For instance, the 5% critical value for the model with intercept is approximately −2.86
(compared to −1.645 for the standard normal).
Lag Length Selection
The choice of p, the number of lagged difference terms, is
critical for the size and power of the ADF test. Common approaches include:
• Information Criteria: Minimize AIC, BIC, or Hannan-Quinn (HQ) over a grid
of candidate lag lengths. Ng and Perron (2001) recommend Modified AIC (MAIC)
for unit root testing.
• Sequential Testing (General-to-Specific): Start with a large pmax (e.g., pmax =
⌊12(T/100)1/4⌋) and sequentially eliminate insignificant lags using t-tests (Hall,
1994).
• Data-Dependent Methods: Ng and Perron (2001) and Schwert (1989) provide
simulation-based guidance.
Alternative Unit Root Tests
• Phillips-Perron (PP) Test (Phillips, 1988): Employs a nonparametric correction to the Dickey-Fuller test statistics to account for serial correlation and heteroskedasticity, obviating the need to specify lag length. The test statistics are
modified versions of the Z(tˆγ) and Z(ˆγ) statistics.
48

• DF-GLS Test (Elliott and Stock, 1996): Applies GLS detrending before performing the ADF regression, substantially improving power, especially when the series
has a large autoregressive root close to unity.
• KPSS Test (Kwiatkowski and Shin, 1992): Reverses the null and alternative hypotheses. H0 is (trend) stationarity against H1 of a unit root. The KPSS statistic
is a Lagrange Multiplier (LM) test based on the partial sum of residuals:
KPSS =
1
T 2ˆσ2
∞
T
X
t=1


t
X
j=1
ˆuj


2
,
(84)
where ˆut are residuals from a regression of Yt on deterministic components, and ˆσ2
∞
is a consistent HAC estimator of the long-run variance.
Confirmatory Analysis: Using both ADF (or DF-GLS) and KPSS tests in conjunction is recommended. A series is conclusively I(1) if ADF fails to reject H0 (unit
root) and KPSS rejects H0 (stationarity). Conversely, I(0) is supported if ADF rejects
and KPSS fails to reject.
5.1.3
Variance Ratio Tests for the Random Walk Hypothesis
Lo and MacKinlay (1988) and Lo and MacKinlay (1989) developed the Variance Ratio
(VR) test for the null hypothesis that log prices follow a random walk. The key insight
is that for a random walk, the variance of q-period returns is exactly q times the variance
of 1-period returns. For a sample of nq +1 log prices {p0, p1, . . . , pnq}, define the q-period
return as Rt(q) = pt −pt−q. The VR statistic is:
VR(q) =
d
Var(Rt(q))
q · d
Var(Rt(1))
.
(85)
Under the random walk null, VR(q) = 1. Values less than 1 indicate mean reversion
(negative autocorrelation), while values greater than 1 indicate momentum (positive autocorrelation). Lo and MacKinlay (1988) developed heteroskedasticity-robust standard
errors using White’s HC covariance estimator. Extended the VR test to multiple horizons
simultaneously using a joint Wald statistic. Wright (2000) proposed nonparametric VR
tests based on ranks and signs that are robust to non-normalities and more powerful than
the standard VR test for heavy-tailed financial data.
5.2
ARIMA Modeling of Conditional Mean
The Autoregressive Integrated Moving Average (ARIMA) framework, pioneered by Box
and Jenkins (1970), provides a parsimonious class of linear models for univariate time
series. While financial returns are largely unpredictable in the mean (consistent with
market efficiency), ARIMA models are essential for modeling expected returns in specific
contexts (e.g., short-term momentum, long-term reversal) and for constructing residuals
for volatility modeling.
49

5.2.1
ARMA Processes: Theoretical Properties
The ARMA(p,q) process for a zero-mean series {Yt} is:
Yt =
p
X
i=1
ϕiYt−i + ϵt +
q
X
j=1
θjϵt−j,
(86)
where {ϵt} is a white noise sequence with E[ϵt] = 0 and Var(ϵt) = σ2. Employing the lag
operator L (LkYt = Yt−k), the model is compactly written as:
ϕ(L)Yt = θ(L)ϵt,
(87)
where ϕ(L) = 1−ϕ1L−· · ·−ϕpLp and θ(L) = 1+θ1L+· · ·+θqLq are the autoregressive
and moving average polynomials, respectively.
Theorem 5.1 (Stationarity and Invertibility Conditions). The ARMA(p,q) process is:
1. Stationary (and causal) if all roots of the characteristic equation ϕ(z) = 0 lie
strictly outside the unit circle (|z| > 1). Under this condition, Yt possesses the
causal MA(∞) representation Yt = ϕ(L)−1θ(L)ϵt = P∞
j=0 ψjϵt−j with absolutely
summable coefficients P |ψj| < ∞.
2. Invertible if all roots of θ(z) = 0 lie strictly outside the unit circle.
Invertibility guarantees that ϵt can be expressed as a convergent AR(∞) process: ϵt =
θ(L)−1ϕ(L)Yt =
P∞
j=0 πjYt−j, which is essential for prediction.
The autocovariance function γh = Cov(Yt, Yt−h) of an ARMA process satisfies the
Yule-Walker equations:
γh = ϕ1γh−1 + · · · + ϕpγh−p,
h ≥max(p, q + 1).
(88)
For h ≤q, additional terms involving θj and σ2 appear. The ACF of an ARMA process
decays exponentially or follows a damped sine wave for h > q −p.
5.2.2
Model Identification, Estimation, and Diagnostic Checking
The Box-Jenkins methodology (Box and Jenkins, 1970) comprises three iterative stages:
Stage 1: Identification. The orders p and q are tentatively selected by examining
the sample ACF and Partial ACF (PACF). The PACF at lag h, denoted ϕhh, is the
correlation between Yt and Yt−h after removing the linear effects of intervening variables
Yt−1, . . . , Yt−h+1. Table 3 summarizes the theoretical patterns.
Table 3: Theoretical ACF and PACF Patterns for ARMA Models
Model

PACF (ϕhh)
AR(p)
Tails off
Cuts off at p

Cuts off at q
Tails off
ARMA(p,q) Tails off after max(0, q −p) Tails off after max(0, p −q)
In practice, sample ACF/PACF are subject to sampling variability, and visual inspection is supplemented by information criteria.
50

Stage 2: Estimation. Given p and q, parameters ϕ, θ, σ2 are typically estimated
via Maximum Likelihood (assuming Gaussian innovations) or Conditional Least Squares.
The exact likelihood for ARMA models can be evaluated efficiently using the Kalman filter
in state-space representation. For pure AR models, OLS is consistent and asymptotically
efficient.
Stage 3: Diagnostic Checking. The adequacy of the fitted model is assessed by
examining the residuals ˆϵt:
• Ljung-Box Q-test (Ljung and Box, 1978): Tests the null that the first m autocorrelations of residuals are zero. Q(m) = T(T + 2)
Pm
k=1 ˆρ2
k/(T −k)
d−→χ2
m−p−q.
• ARCH-LM test (Engle, 1982): Tests for remaining conditional heteroskedasticity
in residuals.
• Normality tests (Jarque-Bera): Assesses distributional assumption.
Stage 4: Model Selection. If multiple candidate models pass diagnostics, information criteria guide selection:
AIC = −2ℓ(ˆθ) + 2k,
(89)
BIC = −2ℓ(ˆθ) + k log T,
(90)
where k = p + q + 1 (or +1 if intercept included). BIC is consistent (selects true order
with probability →1 as T →∞), while AIC is asymptotically efficient for prediction
in the sense of minimizing mean squared prediction error Hannan and Quinn (1979).
recommend BIC for AR order selection. For financial applications where T is large, BIC
tends to select more parsimonious models.
5.3
Volatility Modeling: The GARCH Family
Engle (1982)’s Autoregressive Conditional Heteroskedasticity (ARCH) model and Bollerslev (1986)’s Generalized ARCH (GARCH) extension revolutionized financial econometrics by providing a tractable framework for modeling time-varying volatility. The empirical motivation is the well-documented phenomenon of volatility clustering: large
price changes tend to be followed by large changes (of either sign), and small changes by
small changes, inducing positive serial correlation in squared returns (Mandelbrot, 1963;
Fama, 1965).
5.3.1
ARCH and GARCH: Specification and Properties
The GARCH(p,q) model for the conditional variance σ2

Stationarity and Persistence
The GARCH(1,1) process is covariance stationary if

E[σ2

The sum α + β measures volatility persistence: the rate at which shocks to volatility
decay. For daily equity index returns, estimates typically yield α + β ≈0.99, indicating
near-integrated GARCH (IGARCH) behavior, with volatility shocks exhibiting extremely
long half-lives Nelson (1990), showed that IGARCH models (α + β = 1) are strictly
stationary but not covariance stationary.

5.3.2
Asymmetric GARCH Models: Capturing the Leverage Effect
Black (1976) first documented the leverage effect: negative equity returns are associated with larger subsequent volatility increases than positive returns of equal magnitude.
Asymmetric GARCH models incorporate this stylized fact.
EGARCH (Exponential GARCH) (Nelson, 1991) models the log-variance, circumventing positivity constraints and directly parameterizing the asymmetric response:

Threshold GARCH (TGARCH) and Component GARCH provide further
flexibility in modeling asymmetry and long-run/short-run volatility components (Engle
and Smith, 1999; Zakoian, 1994).
5.3.3
Long Memory in Volatility: FIGARCH and Extensions
While GARCH models imply exponential decay of volatility autocorrelations, empirical
autocorrelations of squared returns and realized variances often exhibit hyperbolic decay
consistent with long memory or fractional integration (Ding et al., 1993; Baillie,
1996). A long memory process has autocorrelations ρh ∼Ch2d−1 as h →∞for d ∈
(0, 0.5), which are not absolutely summable.
FIGARCH (Fractionally Integrated GARCH) (Baillie and Mikkelsen, 1996)
generalizes IGARCH by allowing the differencing parameter d to take fractional values:
σ2
t = ω +
h

IGARCH. The fractional differencing operator (1 −L)d = P∞
k=0
Γ(k−d)
Γ(−d)Γ(k+1)Lk introduces
an infinite distributed lag with hyperbolic decay.
Estimation Challenges: FIGARCH models are computationally demanding due
to the infinite-order representation. Truncation after a large number of lags (e.g., 1000)
or frequency-domain Whittle estimation (Baillie and Mikkelsen, 1996) are standard approaches.
HYAPARCH and FIAPARCH combine long memory, power transformations, and
asymmetry, providing maximum flexibility at the cost of complex estimation (Davidson,
2004).
5.3.4
Realized GARCH and HAR: Leveraging High-Frequency Data
The proliferation of high-frequency intraday data has enabled the construction of precise realized measures of volatility, such as Realized Variance (RV), Realized Kernel
(RK), and Realized Bipower Variation (RBV). These measures serve as accurate ex-post
estimates of integrated variance and can be incorporated into volatility models.
Realized GARCH (Hansen and Shek, 2012; Hansen and Voev, 2016) provides a
joint modeling framework for returns {rt} and a realized measure {xt}:

HAR (Heterogeneous Autoregressive) Model (Corsi, 2009) captures long memory in realized volatility through a parsimonious additive cascade of volatilities over different horizons:
RVt,t+h = c + β(d)RVt−1,t + β(w)RVt−5,t + β(m)RVt−22,t + ϵt+h,
(103)
where RVt−k,t denotes the average realized variance over the past k days. The HAR
model is not a formal long memory process but approximates hyperbolic decay remarkably well with only three parameters. Extensions include HAR-J (incorporating jumps),
HAR-Q (including quarticity), and HAR with leverage and signed jump components
(Corsi, 2012). Bollerslev (2018) provide a comprehensive review of recent developments
in realized volatility forecasting.
5.4
Multivariate GARCH: Modeling Conditional Covariances
Portfolio allocation, risk management, and asset pricing hinge on accurate estimates of
time-varying conditional covariance matrices. Multivariate GARCH (MGARCH) models extend univariate volatility dynamics to the vector case rt ∈RN, with conditional
covariance matrix Ht = Var(rt|Ft−1). The key challenges are: (i) ensuring Ht is positive definite, (ii) parameter parsimony as the number of parameters in an unrestricted
MGARCH model is O(N 4), and (iii) computational feasibility for N > 10.
5.4.1
Dynamic Conditional Correlation (DCC)
Engle (2002) proposed the DCC-GARCH model, which decomposes Ht into conditional
standard deviations and a dynamic correlation matrix:
Ht = DtRtDt,
(104)
where Dt = diag(σ1t, . . . , σNt) with each σ2
it following a univariate GARCH process. The
correlation matrix Rt is modeled as:
Rt = Q∗−1/2

where zt = D−1

govern correlation dynamics. The DCC model is estimated in two steps: (1) univariate
GARCH estimation, (2) correlation targeting for DCC parameters, which dramatically
reduces computational burden and permits estimation for moderately large N (e.g., 100
assets).
Cappiello (2006) introduced the Asymmetric DCC (ADCC) model, incorporating
a leverage effect in correlations via an indicator term nt = 1{rt<0} ⊙rt.

5.4.2
BEKK and GO-GARCH: Alternative Parameterizations
BEKK (Baba-Engle-Kraft-Kroner) (Engle, 1995) specifies Ht directly:
Ht = CTC + ATrt−1rT
t−1A + BTHt−1B,
(107)
where C is upper triangular, and A, B are N × N parameter matrices.
The BEKK
parameterization guarantees positive definiteness by construction. However, the number
of parameters grows quadratically with N, limiting BEKK to small systems (N ≤10).
Diagonal BEKK (A, B diagonal) imposes zero volatility and covariance spillovers, greatly
reducing parameters.
GO-GARCH (Generalized Orthogonal GARCH) (van der Weide, 2002) employs Independent Component Analysis (ICA) to transform the return vector into a set
of statistically independent factors:
rt = Zft,
(108)
where Z is an invertible mixing matrix and ft are independent factors, each following a univariate GARCH process. The conditional covariance is Ht = ZHf
t ZT, where
Hf
t = diag(σ2
f1,t, . . . , σ2
fN,t). GO-GARCH is computationally attractive for large N and
ensures positive definiteness. Estimation typically proceeds via nonlinear least squares
or maximum likelihood (Boswijk and van der Weide, 2012).
Dynamic Equicorrelation (DECO) (Engle and Kelly, 2012) imposes a common
correlation ρt across all asset pairs, drastically reducing parameters for large N applications.
5.5
Cointegration and Pairs Trading
Cointegration, introduced by Engle and Granger (1987), formalizes the concept of longrun equilibrium relationships among non-stationary variables. In finance, cointegration
underpins statistical arbitrage strategies such as pairs trading, where a spread between
two cointegrated assets is mean-reverting.
5.5.1
Definition and Economic Interpretation
Definition 5.3 (Cointegration). An N-dimensional vector time series Yt is cointegrated of order (d, b), denoted Yt ∼CI(d, b), if:
1. All components of Yt are integrated of order d, i.e., Yt ∼I(d).
2. There exists a non-zero cointegrating vector β ∈RN such that the linear combination ut = βTYt is integrated of order d −b, with b > 0.
Typically, d = 1 and b = 1, so that Yt ∼I(1) and βTYt ∼I(0).
The space
spanned by the r linearly independent cointegrating vectors is the cointegrating space.
Cointegration implies the existence of a long-run equilibrium relationship: βTYt = 0
defines the equilibrium, and deviations ut are stationary and mean-reverting.
5.5.2
Engle-Granger Two-Step Procedure
For bivariate systems (N = 2), Engle and Granger (1987) proposed a simple two-step
procedure:
55

1. Step 1:
Estimate the cointegrating regression.
Regress Y1t on Y2t (and
possibly a constant and trend) via OLS:
Y1t = α + βY2t + ut.
(109)
2. Step 2: Test residuals for unit root. Apply the ADF test (without intercept)
to the estimated residuals ˆut:
∆ˆut = γˆut−1 +
p
X
i=1
δi∆ˆut−i + ϵt.
(110)
Reject H0 : γ = 0 in favor of H1 : γ < 0 to conclude cointegration.
Critical Values: Because ˆut are generated regressors, the standard Dickey-Fuller
critical values are invalid. Phillips and Ouliaris (1990) and MacKinnon (2010) provide
appropriate critical values that depend on the number of regressors and deterministic
components. For the bivariate case with intercept, the 5% critical value is approximately
−3.34 (compared to −2.86 for standard ADF).
Limitations: The Engle-Granger procedure (i) can identify at most one cointegrating
relationship, (ii) is sensitive to the normalization (choice of dependent variable), and (iii)
has low power in finite samples.
5.5.3
Johansen’s Maximum Likelihood Framework
Johansen (1988) and Johansen (1991) developed a unified maximum likelihood approach
for estimating and testing multiple cointegrating vectors in a Vector Error Correction
Model (VECM). The VECM representation is:
∆Yt = ΠYt−1 +
p−1
X
i=1
Γi∆Yt−i + ΦDt + ϵt,
(111)
where Π = αβT. The matrix β (N × r) contains the r cointegrating vectors, and α
(N × r) contains the adjustment coefficients, which measure the speed at which each
variable corrects toward equilibrium. The rank of Π, denoted r, equals the number of
cointegrating relationships.
Trace Test: Tests H0 : r = r0 against H1 : r > r0 (or r = N):
λtrace(r0) = −T
N
X
i=r0+1
log(1 −ˆλi),
(112)
where ˆλi are the squared canonical correlations (eigenvalues) from the reduced rank regression.
Maximum Eigenvalue Test: Tests H0 : r = r0 against H1 : r = r0 + 1:
λmax(r0) = −T log(1 −ˆλr0+1).
(113)
Critical values, tabulated by Johansen (1995) and Osterwald-Lenum (1992), depend
on the deterministic specification (no constant, restricted constant, unrestricted constant,
trend).
56

5.5.4
Error Correction Model and Pairs Trading
The Granger Representation Theorem (Engle and Granger, 1987) establishes that any
cointegrated system admits a VECM representation, and conversely, any VECM generates
cointegrated series. The VECM explicitly decomposes dynamics into:
• Long-run equilibrium: βTYt−1 are stationary error correction terms.
• Short-run dynamics: Γi capture transitory adjustments.
Pairs Trading Strategy: Gatev and Rouwenhorst (2006) and Vidyamurthy (2004)
popularized statistical arbitrage based on cointegration. For a cointegrated pair (S1t, S2t)
with cointegrating vector β = (1, −β)T, the spread ut = S1t −βS2t is stationary. The
trading rule is:
• Enter a long-short position when |ut| exceeds k standard deviations (e.g., k = 2).
• Unwind when ut reverts to its mean.
Do (2010) and Avellaneda (2010) demonstrate the profitability of cointegration-based
strategies, while Huck (2010) and Krauss (2017) extend to higher-dimensional baskets.
5.6
State Space Models and the Kalman Filter
State space representations provide a unified framework for modeling a vast array of
time series models, including ARIMA, unobserved components, stochastic volatility, and
time-varying parameter regressions. The Kalman filter (Kalman, 1960) delivers optimal
recursive estimates of the unobserved state vector.
5.6.1
Linear Gaussian State Space Model
The general formulation is:
αt+1 = Ttαt + Rtηt,
ηt ∼N(0, Qt),
(114)
yt = Ztαt + ϵt,
ϵt ∼N(0, Ht),
(115)
where yt is the observed vector, αt is the unobserved state vector, and ηt, ϵt are mutually
independent. The system matrices Tt, Rt, Qt, Zt, Ht may depend on unknown parameters
ψ.
5.6.2
The Kalman Filter Algorithm
The Kalman filter recursively computes the conditional mean at|t = E[αt|Ft] and covariance Pt|t = Var(αt|Ft) of the state vector given observations up to time t.

5.6.3
Financial Applications
• Dynamic Factor Models: State vector contains latent factors (e.g., market, size,
value) that drive asset returns. The Kalman filter estimates factor realizations and
loadings (Stock and Watson, 1991; Bai and Wang, 2014).
• Time-Varying Beta: State βt follows random walk; observation is CAPM regression rt = α + βtrmt + ϵt (Adrian et al., 2015).
• Stochastic Volatility: State αt = log σ2
t follows AR(1); observation rt = exp(αt/2)zt
is non-Gaussian.
The Kalman filter is combined with importance sampling or
MCMC (Harvey and Shephard, 1994; Kim and Chib, 1998).
• Pairs Trading with Time-Varying Hedge: State βt is dynamic hedge ratio;
observation is spread yt = S1t −βtS2t (Elliott, 2005).
5.7
High-Frequency Market Microstructure Econometrics
The availability of tick-by-tick transaction and quote data has spawned a specialized
econometric literature addressing the unique characteristics of high-frequency financial
data (Hautsch, 2011).
5.7.1
Stylized Facts of Tick Data
High-frequency data exhibit distinct features absent from daily data:
• Irregular Temporal Spacing: Transactions and quote updates occur at random,
non-equidistant times.
• Diurnal (Intraday) Patterns: Volatility, volume, and spreads follow a deterministic U-shaped pattern: high at market open and close, lower mid-day.
• Bid-Ask Bounce: Transaction prices oscillate between bid and ask quotes, inducing negative first-order autocorrelation in returns even in efficient markets.
58

• Microstructure Noise: Observed prices are contaminated by transient frictions
(inventory effects, order processing costs, discrete price grid), obscuring the latent
efficient price.
• Price Discreteness: Prices are constrained to a discrete grid of tick sizes, necessitating discrete-valued models.
5.7.2
The Roll Model for Bid-Ask Spread
Roll (1984) proposed a seminal model relating bid-ask bounce to return autocorrelation.
Let Pt be the observed transaction price, bouncing between bid Bt and ask At. Under
the assumption of equally probable buy and sell orders, the effective spread s satisfies:
Cov(∆Pt, ∆Pt−1) = −s2
4 .
(123)
Solving for s yields the Roll spread estimator: ˆs = 2
q
−d
Cov(∆Pt, ∆Pt−1). Extensions
include:
• Glosten-Harris (1988): Decomposes spread into order processing and adverse
selection components.
• Hasbrouck (1991, 1993): Vector autoregressive models of trades and quote revisions.
• Madhavan et al. (1997): Incorporates trade size and information asymmetry.
5.7.3

5.7.4
Jump Detection and Decomposition
Jumps represent large, discontinuous price movements and are critical for risk management and option pricing. Barndorff-Nielsen (2004) introduced Bipower Variation
(BPV), which is robust to jumps:

5.8
ACD Models for Transaction Durations
Engle and Russell (1998) introduced the Autoregressive Conditional Duration (ACD)
model for analyzing irregularly spaced transaction data. Let ti be the arrival time of the
i-th event (trade, quote update), and xi = ti −ti−1 be the duration between events. The
ACD model specifies the conditional mean duration ψi = E[xi|Fi−1]:
xi = ψiϵi,
ϵi
i.i.d.
∼
with positive support and E[ϵi] = 1.
(129)
The standard ACD(p,q) specification is:
ψi = ω +
p
X
j=1
αjxi−j +
q
X
k=1
βkψi−k.
(130)
Common distributions for ϵi include Exponential (EACD), Weibull (WACD), Gamma
(GACD), and Burr. ACD models capture clustering in trading activity: short durations
tend to be followed by short durations, and vice versa.
Extensions:
60

• Log-ACD: log ψi = ω + P αj log xi−j + P βk log ψi−k ensures positivity without
parameter constraints.
• SCD (Stochastic Conditional Duration): Introduces a latent stochastic process for ψi, estimated via Kalman filter or MCMC (Bauwens, 2004).
• Markov-Switching ACD: Accommodates regime changes in trading intensity.
ACD models are essential for modeling trade arrival processes, estimating transaction
costs, and constructing intraday volatility signatures (Engle, 2000).
6
Portfolio Statistics and Factor Models
Portfolio theory constitutes the normative framework for investment decision-making
under uncertainty, translating statistical estimates of return distributions, covariances,
and factor exposures into optimal asset allocations. Since the seminal contribution of
Markowitz (1952), which established mean-variance analysis as the cornerstone of modern
portfolio theory, the field has evolved to address the formidable statistical challenges
inherent in high-dimensional financial data: the curse of dimensionality in covariance
matrix estimation, the severe impact of estimation error on portfolio weights, the timevarying nature of risk premia and volatilities, and the necessity of incorporating economic
structure through factor models. This section provides a comprehensive treatment of
portfolio statistics, progressing from fundamental return and risk measurement through
advanced covariance estimation and factor modeling to robust portfolio optimization
techniques.
6.1
Return and Risk Measurement
6.1.1
Return Conventions and Aggregation
The accurate measurement of asset returns is foundational to portfolio analysis. Two
primary conventions govern return calculations:
Simple (Discrete) Return: For an asset with price Pt at time t, the one-period
simple return is:
Rt = Pt −Pt−1

in the Black-Scholes framework. However, log returns do not aggregate linearly across

Return Conventions in Practice: For short-horizon (daily, weekly) and moderatereturn environments, the difference between simple and log returns is negligible. However,
for longer horizons or volatile assets, the approximation error can be material. Portfolio
optimization is typically conducted using simple returns for consistency with the linear aggregation property, while risk models often employ log returns for their temporal
stability and approximate normality.
6.1.2
Risk Metrics: From Variance to Coherent Measures
Risk measurement quantifies the uncertainty or potential loss associated with an investment.
The choice of risk metric profoundly influences portfolio construction and
regulatory capital requirements.
Variance and Standard Deviation: Variance, defined as the second central moment σ2 = E[(R −µ)2] with µ = E[R], is the traditional risk measure of mean-variance
analysis. Its mathematical tractability and the fact that it fully characterizes risk under
multivariate normality have cemented its central role. However, variance treats upside
and downside deviations symmetrically, penalizing positive surprises equally with negative ones—a feature inconsistent with investor preferences. Moreover, variance fails to
capture tail risk adequately for heavy-tailed return distributions ubiquitous in finance.
Semi-Variance and Downside Risk: Markowitz (1959) himself advocated for semivariance, which considers only deviations below a target threshold τ (often the mean or
a minimum acceptable return):
σ2
−(τ) = E
h
min(R −τ, 0)2i
.
(135)
Semi-variance aligns with the behavioral observation that investors are averse to losses
but not to gains. Extensions include the Sortino ratio, which replaces standard deviation
with downside deviation in the Sharpe ratio.
Value-at-Risk (VaR): VaR emerged as the industry standard for market risk measurement and regulatory capital under the Basel Accords. For a given confidence level
α ∈(0, 1) (typically 0.95 or 0.99), VaRα is the α-quantile of the loss distribution:
VaRα = inf{ℓ∈R : P(L > ℓ) ≤1 −α} = F −1
L (α),
(136)
where L = −R denotes the loss (negative return).
VaR is intuitive and widely implemented, but suffers from critical theoretical deficiencies: it is not a coherent risk
measure as defined by Artzner and Heath (1999), specifically violating the subadditivity axiom. Subadditivity requires that the risk of a combined position not exceed
the sum of individual risks: ρ(X + Y ) ≤ρ(X) + ρ(Y ), encapsulating the diversification
62

principle. VaR can violate subadditivity for non-elliptical distributions, implying that
diversification may appear to increase risk—a perverse incentive for risk aggregation.
Expected Shortfall (ES) / Conditional VaR (CVaR): Expected Shortfall, also
known as Conditional Value-at-Risk (CVaR) or Tail Value-at-Risk (TVaR), addresses
VaR’s lack of coherence and tail insensitivity. ESα is the expected loss conditional on the
loss exceeding VaRα:
ESα = E[L|L > VaRα] =
1
1 −α
Z 1
α VaRu du.
(137)
Acerbi and Tasche (2002b) proved that ES is a coherent risk measure, satisfying subadditivity, monotonicity, positive homogeneity, and translation invariance. Furthermore, ES
is sensitive to the severity of losses beyond the VaR threshold, capturing tail risk that
VaR ignores. Reflecting these advantages, the Basel Committee on Banking Supervision
replaced VaR with ES (at the 97.5% confidence level) for market risk capital requirements under the Fundamental Review of the Trading Book (FRTB) (Basel Committee
on Banking Supervision, 2019).
Maximum Drawdown and Path-Dependent Risk: For performance evaluation
and hedge fund risk management, path-dependent measures such as Maximum Drawdown
(MDD) are paramount. MDD is the maximum observed loss from a peak to a trough
over a specified horizon:
MDDT = max
0≤t≤T
 max0≤s≤t Ps −Pt
max0≤s≤t Ps
!
.
(138)
MDD captures the worst-case cumulative loss an investor would have experienced, reflecting both volatility and serial correlation. The Calmar ratio (annualized return divided
by MDD) and the Sterling ratio are common drawdown-adjusted performance metrics.
Entropic Risk Measures and Divergence: In incomplete markets and robust
portfolio optimization, entropic risk measures ρ(X) = 1
γ log E[e−γX] (for returns X) provide a connection to exponential utility and relative entropy constraints (Föllmer and
Schied, 2016).
6.2
Covariance Matrix Estimation
The covariance matrix of asset returns is the central input to mean-variance optimization,
risk budgeting, and factor model estimation. For an N-dimensional return vector Rt with
mean µ, the population covariance matrix is Σ = E[(Rt −µ)(Rt −µ)T]. In practice, Σ is
unknown and must be estimated from a finite sample of T observations. The challenges
of high-dimensional covariance estimation—where N is comparable to or exceeds T—are
among the most acute in quantitative finance.
6.2.1
Sample Covariance Matrix and Its Pitfalls
The sample covariance matrix is the maximum likelihood estimator under multivariate
normality:
ˆΣS =
1
T −1
T
X
t=1
(Rt −¯R)(Rt −¯R)T.
(139)
While unbiased and consistent for fixed N as T →∞, ˆΣS exhibits severe pathologies in
high dimensions (N/T →c > 0):
63

1. Ill-Conditioning: The condition number κ( ˆΣS) = λmax/λmin diverges as N/T →
1, rendering the inverse ˆΣ−1
S
unstable and mean-variance weights highly sensitive
to estimation error.
2. Eigenvalue Distortion: The sample eigenvalues are systematically biased: the
largest sample eigenvalues overestimate the true population eigenvalues, while the
smallest underestimate them.
This "eigenvalue spreading" is described by the
Marčenko-Pastur (MP) law.
Marčenko-Pastur Theorem and Random Matrix Theory
Marčenko and Pastur
(1967) characterized the limiting spectral distribution of sample covariance matrices under
the asymptotic regime N, T →∞with N/T →c ∈(0, ∞). For a matrix X ∈RT×N
with i.i.d. entries having mean zero, variance σ2, and finite fourth moment, the empirical
spectral distribution of 1
T XTX converges almost surely to the MP distribution:
fMP(λ) =
1
2πcσ2λ
q
(λ+ −λ)(λ −λ−),
λ ∈[λ−, λ+],
(140)
with support bounds λ± = σ2(1 ± √c)2. The MP law implies that for c > 0, the sample
eigenvalues are confined within [λ−, λ+] even if the population covariance is the identity
matrix (Σ = σ2I). Eigenvalues outside this interval are not attributable to sampling noise
and correspond to genuine factors or structure in the population covariance. This insight
underpins Random Matrix Theory (RMT) denoising methods for covariance estimation.
6.2.2
Linear and Nonlinear Shrinkage Estimation
Shrinkage estimation offers a principled approach to reducing the mean squared error of
the sample covariance matrix by "shrinking" it toward a well-conditioned target matrix.
Linear Shrinkage (Ledoit-Wolf): Ledoit et al. (2004) proposed shrinking the
sample covariance toward a structured target F:
ˆΣLW = δF + (1 −δ) ˆΣS,
(141)
where δ ∈[0, 1] is the shrinkage intensity. The target F is typically chosen as the constantcorrelation matrix with elements fij = σiσj ¯ρ, where ¯ρ is the average pairwise correlation.
The optimal shrinkage intensity δ∗is derived by minimizing the expected Frobenius loss
E[∥ˆΣLW−Σ∥2
F] and can be consistently estimated from the data. Linear shrinkage yields a
well-conditioned estimator that dominates the sample covariance matrix in finite samples.
Nonlinear Shrinkage: Ledoit and Wolf (2017) generalized linear shrinkage by allowing eigenvalue-specific shrinkage intensities. The nonlinear shrinkage estimator applies
an oracle shrinkage function φ(λ) to each sample eigenvalue:
ˆΣNL = Vdiag(φ(λ1), . . . , φ(λp))VT,
(142)
where V is the matrix of sample eigenvectors and φ(λ) is estimated by inverting the
Marčenko-Pastur equation. Nonlinear shrinkage is asymptotically optimal in the Frobenius loss sense and has been shown to deliver superior out-of-sample portfolio performance
compared to linear shrinkage, particularly when N/T is not small.
Adaptive Beta Shrinkage: Staal (2025) introduced adaptive beta shrinkage, which
dynamically adjusts the shrinkage target and intensity based on time-varying market
conditions, improving robustness during regime shifts.
64

6.2.3
Random Matrix Theory Denoising
RMT denoising (Laloux, 2000; Bun, 2017) exploits the MP law to separate signal from
noise in the sample covariance spectrum. The procedure, known as eigenvalue clipping
or eigenvalue cleaning, proceeds as follows:
1. Perform spectral decomposition: ˆΣS = PN
i=1 λivivT
i .
2. Identify eigenvalues exceeding the MP upper bound λ+ as "signal" eigenvalues. The
remaining eigenvalues are attributed to noise.
3. Replace the noise eigenvalues with a constant (e.g., their average) or with values
derived from the MP bulk.
4. Reconstruct the denoised covariance matrix: ˆΣRMT =
PK
i=1 λivivT
i +¯λnoise
PN
i=K+1 vivT
i ,
where K is the number of signal eigenvalues.
RMT denoising dramatically improves the out-of-sample Sharpe ratio of mean-variance
portfolios and enhances risk model accuracy (Bun, 2017).
6.2.4
Graphical LASSO and Sparse Precision Matrix Estimation
In high dimensions, imposing sparsity on the precision (inverse covariance) matrix Θ =
Σ−1 is both statistically regularizing and economically interpretable. Zeros in Θ correspond to conditional independence relationships in the underlying Gaussian graphical
model: Θij = 0 ⇐⇒Ri ⊥⊥Rj | R−{i,j}. The Graphical LASSO (Friedman and Tibshirani, 2008; Yuan and Lin, 2007) estimates Θ by maximizing the ℓ1-penalized Gaussian
log-likelihood:
ˆΘ = arg max

The Graphical LASSO reveals the underlying network structure
of asset returns, identifying clusters of conditionally dependent assets and providing a
parsimonious, well-conditioned covariance matrix estimator.
6.2.5
POET: Principal Orthogonal complEment Thresholding
Fan (2013) introduced POET, a method that leverages the pervasive factor structure in
financial returns. Assume an approximate factor model:
Rt = Bft + ut,
Cov(ft) = ΣF,
Cov(ut) = Σu,
(144)
where ft ∈RK are K common factors (K ≪N), B ∈RN×K is the loading matrix, and Σu
is the idiosyncratic covariance matrix, assumed to be sparse. The population covariance
decomposes as Σ = BΣFBT + Σu. POET estimates Σ by:
1. Extracting the first K principal components from ˆΣS to estimate the common
component ˆΣC =
PK
i=1 ˆλiˆviˆvT
i .
2. Applying adaptive thresholding to the residual covariance matrix ˆΣu = ˆΣS −ˆΣC
to enforce sparsity.
POET is computationally scalable to very large dimensions (e.g., N > 1000) and enjoys
strong theoretical guarantees, including consistency under the Frobenius norm even when
N ≫T.

6.3
Factor Models
Factor models provide a parsimonious description of the covariance structure of asset
returns and form the theoretical bedrock of asset pricing. They posit that the systematic
component of returns is driven by a small number of common factors, with the remaining
idiosyncratic risk diversifiable.
6.3.1
Theoretical Foundation: Arbitrage Pricing Theory
Ross (1976)’s Arbitrage Pricing Theory (APT) establishes that, in the absence of arbitrage and under certain regularity conditions, expected returns are approximately linear
in factor loadings:
E[Ri] = λ0 + βT
i λ,
(145)
where βi ∈RK is the vector of factor exposures (betas) and λ ∈RK is the vector of
factor risk premia. The APT nests the Capital Asset Pricing Model (CAPM) of Sharpe
(1964) and Lintner (1965) as the special case with a single factor (the market portfolio).
The statistical factor model corresponding to APT is:
Rt = α + Bft + ϵt,
E[ϵt|ft] = 0,
Cov(ϵt) = Ψ (diagonal),
(146)
implying the covariance decomposition Σ = BΣFBT + Ψ.
6.3.2
Empirical Factor Models
Decades of empirical research have identified characteristic-based factors that explain the
cross-section of expected stock returns. Table 4 summarizes the canonical models.

Factor Construction: Factors are typically constructed as long-short portfolios
formed by sorting stocks on characteristics. For example, SMB (Small Minus Big) is the
return difference between small-cap and large-cap portfolios; HML (High Minus Low)
is the return difference between high book-to-market (value) and low book-to-market
(growth) portfolios.
66

Factor Zoo and Replication Crisis: The proliferation of candidate factors—dubbed
the "factor zoo" (Cochrane, 2011; Harvey and Zhu, 2016)—has raised concerns about data
snooping and the replicability of published anomalies. Harvey and Zhu (2016) argue that
multiple testing adjustments (e.g., controlling the False Discovery Rate) and higher statistical hurdles (e.g., t-statistic > 3.0) are necessary to separate genuine risk premia from
spurious findings.
6.3.3
Statistical Factor Models: Principal Component Analysis
When factor realizations are unobservable, Principal Component Analysis (PCA) provides a data-driven method for extracting statistical factors. Given the sample covariance
matrix ˆΣS, the eigendecomposition yields:

The number of factors K can be

2013), or RMT-based methods (Laloux, 2000). Empirically, the first PC explains 30–40%
of total variance and is highly correlated with the market factor; subsequent PCs capture
industry and style effects (Connor and Korajczyk, 1988).
6.4
Portfolio Optimization
Portfolio optimization formalizes the trade-off between risk and expected return. The
classical Markowitz mean-variance framework provides the normative solution, but its
practical implementation is plagued by estimation error, necessitating robust extensions.
6.4.1
Mean-Variance Optimization and the Efficient Frontier
Markowitz (1952) formulated the portfolio selection problem as a quadratic program. For
a given target expected return µ0, the minimum-variance portfolio is:
min

w∗(µ0) = C −µ0B

(151)
where A = 1TΣ−11, B = 1TΣ−1µ, and C = µTΣ−1µ. The efficient frontier is the
set of portfolios that minimize variance for each level of expected return. The global
minimum variance portfolio (GMVP) corresponds to wGMVP = Σ−11/A, independent
of µ. The tangency portfolio (maximum Sharpe ratio) is wtan ∝Σ−1µ (with excess
returns).
67

6.4.2
The Estimation Error Problem and the Michaud Critique
Michaud (1989) famously characterized mean-variance optimization as an "estimationerror maximizer." The optimal weights w∗are highly sensitive to errors in estimating µ
and Σ. Specifically:
• Assets with overestimated expected returns and underestimated variances/covariances
receive disproportionately large positive weights.
• Assets with underestimated expected returns and overestimated risks receive extreme negative weights (in unconstrained optimization).
The consequence is that out-of-sample performance of mean-variance portfolios often lags
naive benchmarks such as the equally-weighted portfolio (DeMiguel and Uppal, 2007).
The degradation is particularly severe when N is large relative to T.
Mitigation Strategies:
1. Shrinkage of Expected Returns: Shrinking sample means toward a common
target (e.g., the grand mean or a factor-model implied mean) reduces estimation
error (Jorion, 1986).
2. Covariance Shrinkage: Replacing ˆΣS with a shrunk estimator (Ledoit-Wolf,
nonlinear shrinkage) improves conditioning.
3. Constraints: Imposing non-negativity constraints (wi ≥0) or upper bounds prevents extreme weights and improves out-of-sample performance (Jagannathan and
Ma, 2003).
4. Bayesian Approaches: The Black-Litterman model (see below) provides a coherent Bayesian framework.
6.4.3
The Black-Litterman Model
Black and Litterman (1992) developed a Bayesian approach to portfolio optimization that
addresses the sensitivity to expected return estimates. The model combines two sources
of information:
1. Prior (Equilibrium Returns): Derived from the Capital Asset Pricing Model,
the implied equilibrium returns are Π = γΣwmkt, where γ is the market risk aversion coefficient and wmkt are market capitalization weights. The prior distribution
is µ ∼N(Π, τΣ).
2. Investor Views: Subjective views on linear combinations of returns are expressed
as Pµ = Q+ϵ, with ϵ ∼N(0, Ω). The matrix P selects the assets involved in each
view, Q quantifies the expected return difference, and Ωreflects view uncertainty.
Applying Bayes’ theorem, the posterior distribution of expected returns is µ|Views ∼
N(ˆµBL, ˆΣBL), where:
ˆµBL =
h
(τΣ)−1 + PTΩ−1P
i−1 h
(τΣ)−1Π + PTΩ−1Q

(152)
ˆΣBL =

(153)
68

The Black-Litterman optimal portfolio is then w∗

intuitive, well-diversified portfolios that deviate from market weights only in directions
supported by strong views. Sanhueza (2025) extend the framework to incorporate dynamic betas and regime-switching volatility via MCMC estimation.
6.4.4

Teïletche, 2010) allocates capital such that each asset contributes equally to the overall
portfolio risk.

7
Stochastic Processes for Derivative Pricing
The modern theory of derivative pricing rests upon the mathematical edifice of continuoustime stochastic processes and the associated calculus of Itô integration. Since the seminal contributions of Black and Scholes (1973) and Merton (1973), which established the
arbitrage-free pricing of contingent claims in a Brownian motion-driven market, stochastic
calculus has become the lingua franca of quantitative finance. The fundamental theorem
of asset pricing (Harrison and Kreps, 1979; Delbaen and Schachermayer, 1994) establishes an equivalence between the absence of arbitrage and the existence of an equivalent martingale measure (risk-neutral measure) under which discounted asset prices are
martingales. This section provides a rigorous treatment of the probabilistic machinery
underlying continuous-time finance, progressing from Brownian motion and Itô calculus
through essential stochastic differential equations to jump processes, stochastic volatility,
regime-switching, and affine models.
69

7.1
Brownian Motion and Its Properties
Brownian motion (or the Wiener process) is the fundamental building block of continuoustime stochastic models. Its mathematical construction, due to Wiener (1923), provides a
rigorous foundation for modeling the random evolution of asset prices.
Definition 7.1 (Standard Brownian Motion). A stochastic process {Wt}t≥0 defined on
a filtered probability space (Ω, F, {Ft}t≥0, P) is a standard Brownian motion (or
Wiener process) if:
1. W0 = 0 almost surely.
2. Independent Increments: For any 0 ≤s < t, the increment Wt −Ws is independent of the σ-algebra Fs.
3. Stationary Gaussian Increments: For any 0 ≤s < t, Wt −Ws ∼N(0, t −s).
4. Continuity of Sample Paths: With probability one, the mapping t 7→Wt(ω) is
continuous.
Measure-Theoretic Construction
The existence of Brownian motion is non-trivial.
Wiener (1923) constructed Wt as a random Fourier series with independent Gaussian
coefficients, establishing a probability measure on the space of continuous functions
C[0, ∞)—the Wiener measure. Karatzas and Shreve (2012) and Revuz and Yor (1999)
provide comprehensive treatments of Brownian motion and its properties.
Path Properties and Quadratic Variation
Despite the continuity of sample paths,
Brownian motion exhibits remarkable irregularity:
• Non-Differentiability: With probability one, the sample path t 7→Wt is nowhere
differentiable. The Paley and Zygmund (1933) and Zygmund (1959) theorem establishes that Brownian paths are Hölder continuous of order α < 1/2 but not of
order α ≥1/2.
• Unbounded Variation: The total variation of Wt on any finite interval is infinite almost surely: supΠ
Pn
i=1 |Wti −Wti−1| = ∞, where the supremum is over all
partitions Π of [0, T].
• Quadratic Variation: The quadratic variation process [W, W]t is defined as the
limit in probability:
[W, W]t = lim
∥Π∥→0
n
X
i=1
(Wti −Wti−1)2 = t.
(156)
The fact that [W, W]t = t (and not zero, as would be the case for a differentiable
function) is the source of the Itô correction term in stochastic calculus.
Martingale Properties
Brownian motion is a continuous martingale with respect to
its natural filtration: E[Wt|Fs] = Ws for 0 ≤s ≤t. Moreover, the processes W 2
t −t,
exp(σWt −1
2σ2t), and W 3
t −3tWt are also martingales, a fact extensively exploited in
option pricing and stochastic analysis.
70

7.2
Itô Calculus
The development of a stochastic integral with respect to Brownian motion, due to Itô
(1944, 1951), resolved the fundamental challenge that the non-differentiability of Wt
precludes pathwise Riemann-Stieltjes integration.

7.2.2
Itô’s Lemma: The Fundamental Theorem of Stochastic Calculus
Itô’s lemma is the stochastic analog of the chain rule for deterministic calculus. It provides
the rule for differentiating a function of an Itô process.

Geometric Brownian Motion and the Black-Scholes Model
The canonical model for asset prices in continuous time is Geometric Brownian Motion

Risk-Neutral Pricing and the Black-Scholes PDE
The Fundamental Theorem
of Asset Pricing (Harrison and Kreps, 1979) asserts that in an arbitrage-free market,
there exists an equivalent probability measure Q (the risk-neutral measure) under which
discounted asset prices are martingales. Under Q, the drift of St is the risk-free rate r:
dSt = rStdt + σStdW Q
t ,
(168)
where W Q

T, the arbitrage-free price at time t is:
Vt = e−r(T−t)EQ[H(ST)|Ft].
(169)

Black and Scholes (1973) solved this PDE for European call and put options, yielding
the celebrated Black-Scholes formula.
7.3
Key Stochastic Differential Equations in Finance
Beyond Geometric Brownian Motion, a rich array of SDEs have been developed to capture
the empirical features of financial time series: mean reversion in interest rates, stochastic
volatility, and heavy-tailed returns.
7.3.1
Ornstein-Uhlenbeck Process and the Vasicek Model
The Ornstein-Uhlenbeck (OU) process is the canonical mean-reverting Gaussian process.
Vasicek (1977) applied the OU process to model the short-term interest rate rt:
drt = κ(θ −rt)dt + σdWt,
r0 ∈R.
(171)
The parameter κ > 0 governs the speed of mean reversion, θ is the long-run mean level,
and σ is the volatility. The explicit solution is obtained via the integrating factor eκt:
rt = θ + (r0 −θ)e−κt + σ
Z t
0 e−κ(t−s)dWs.
(172)
The transition distribution is Gaussian:
rt|r0 ∼N
 
θ + (r0 −θ)e−κt, σ2
2κ(1 −e−2κt)
!
.
(173)
The Gaussian nature of the Vasicek model permits negative interest rates, which may
be economically undesirable but has become empirically relevant in post-2008 markets.
Zero-coupon bond prices are exponential-affine functions of rt:
P(t, T) = EQ[e−R T
t rsds|Ft] = exp(A(t, T) −B(t, T)rt),
(174)
where B(τ) = 1
κ(1 −e−κτ) and A(τ) is a deterministic function of model parameters.
7.3.2
Cox-Ingersoll-Ross (CIR) Model
To preclude negative interest rates, Cox (1985) proposed the CIR model, in which volatility is proportional to the square root of the rate:
drt = κ(θ −rt)dt + σ√rtdWt,
r0 > 0.
(175)
The diffusion coefficient σ√rt ensures that the process remains non-negative. Specifically,
if the Feller condition 2κθ ≥σ2 holds, the origin is unattainable and rt > 0 almost
surely. The transition density of rt is a non-central chi-square distribution. The CIR
model also yields exponential-affine bond prices, providing analytical tractability while
maintaining economic plausibility.
73

7.3.3
Heston Stochastic Volatility Model
The assumption of constant volatility in the Black-Scholes model is contradicted by overwhelming empirical evidence of volatility clustering, leverage effects, and implied volatility smiles. Heston (1993) proposed a seminal stochastic volatility model in which the
variance vt = σ2
t follows a CIR process:
dSt = µStdt + √vtStdW S
t ,
(176)
dvt = κ(θ −vt)dt + ξ√vtdW v
t ,
(177)
where W S
t
and W v

t dW v
t ] = ρdt.
The
leverage effect is captured by ρ < 0: negative equity returns are associated with increases
in variance.
The Heston model admits a semi-analytical solution for European option prices via
characteristic function inversion.
Let xt = log St.
The characteristic function of xT
conditional on (xt, vt) is:
ϕ(u) = EQ[eiuxT |xt, vt] = exp (C(T −t, u) + D(T −t, u)vt + iuxt) ,

obtained via Fourier inversion:
C(St, vt, t) = StP1 −Ke−r(T−t)P2,
(179)
where P1 and P2 are tail probabilities computed by integrating the characteristic function
(Carr and Madan, 1999; Lewis, 2000).
7.4
Jump Processes and Lévy Models
Continuous-path diffusion models fail to capture the sudden, discontinuous price movements (jumps) observed in financial markets, particularly during earnings announcements, macroeconomic news releases, and crises. Lévy processes generalize Brownian
motion by incorporating jumps while preserving the property of stationary and independent increments.
7.4.1
Poisson Process and Compound Poisson Process
A Poisson process {Nt}t≥0 with intensity λ > 0 is a counting process satisfying:
1. N0 = 0.
2. Independent increments: Nt −Ns ⊥⊥Fs for 0 ≤s < t.
3. Poisson distributed increments: Nt −Ns ∼Poisson(λ(t −s)).
The probability mass function is P(Nt = k) = e−λt(λt)k/k! for k ∈N0. The Poisson
process models the arrival of jump events.
A compound Poisson process Jt = PNt

7.4.3
Variance Gamma and Normal Inverse Gaussian Models
Pure jump Lévy processes with infinite activity (infinitely many jumps in any finite interval) can generate realistic return distributions without a continuous Brownian component.
Variance Gamma (VG) Process: Introduced by Madan and Seneta (1990) and
Madan and Chang (1998), the VG process is constructed by subordinating a Brownian
motion with drift by a Gamma process. Specifically, Xt = θGt + σWGt, where Gt ∼
Gamma(t/ν, ν) is a Gamma process with mean rate 1/ν and variance rate ν. The VG
process is a pure jump process with infinite activity but finite variation. Its characteristic
function is:
ϕXt(u) =

7.5
Regime-Switching Models
Financial markets exhibit distinct regimes—bull and bear markets, high and low volatility periods, crises and tranquil times—that are not adequately captured by single-regime
models. Regime-switching models, pioneered by Hamilton (1989) in the context of business cycles, allow parameters to shift according to an unobserved Markov chain.
Let st ∈{1, 2, . . . , K} be a latent state variable following a first-order Markov chain
with transition matrix P = [pij], where pij = P(st = j|st−1 = i). Conditional on the
regime, the return distribution is:
rt|st = k ∼N(µk, σ2
k).
(187)
Estimation proceeds via the Hamilton filter, a recursive algorithm for computing filtered probabilities P(st = k|Ft) and smoothed probabilities P(st = k|FT). The likelihood
function is evaluated as a byproduct of the filter and maximized numerically.
Applications in Finance
• Bull and Bear Market Identification: Maheu (2000) apply Markov-switching
models to identify distinct regimes in stock returns, characterizing bull markets by
high mean returns and low volatility, and bear markets by low or negative returns
and high volatility.
• Crisis Detection: Regime-switching models provide a probabilistic framework for
detecting the onset of financial crises and adjusting risk management accordingly.
• Time-Varying Transition Probabilities: Extensions by Diebold and Weinbach
(1994) and Filardo (1994) allow transition probabilities to depend on covariates,
such as leading economic indicators or volatility measures, capturing the notion
that regime shifts are more likely under certain economic conditions.
• Markov-Switching GARCH: Haas and Paolella (2004) and Klaassen (2002)
combine regime-switching with GARCH dynamics to model volatility persistence
within regimes while allowing for abrupt shifts in volatility levels.
7.6
Affine Jump-Diffusions
Duffie and Singleton (2000) developed a unified framework for a broad class of continuoustime models—affine jump-diffusions—in which the drift vector, instantaneous covariance matrix, and jump intensities are affine (linear plus constant) functions of the state
vector. This class encompasses the vast majority of tractable models in finance, including
Vasicek, CIR, Heston, and their multi-dimensional extensions with jumps.
Consider an n-dimensional state vector Xt satisfying:
dXt = µ(Xt)dt + σ(Xt)dWt + dJt,
(188)
µ(x) = K0 + K1x,
(189)
(σ(x)σ(x)T)ij = (H0)ij + (H1)ij · x,
(190)
where K0, K1, H0, H1 are coefficient matrices of appropriate dimensions. The jump component Jt is a compound Poisson process with intensity λ(x) = ℓ0 + ℓT
1 x and jump-size
76

distribution with moment generating function ψ(u) = E[euT ∆J]. The short rate (or discount rate) is also affine: r(x) = ρ0 + ρT
1 x.
Under these affine assumptions, the conditional characteristic function (or discounted
transform) of XT is exponentially affine in the state vector:
E

e−R T
t r(Xs)dseuT XT
Ft

= exp

A(T −t, u) + B(T −t, u)TXt

(191)
where A(τ, u) and B(τ, u) satisfy a system of (generalized) Riccati ordinary differential
equations. This remarkable property enables quasi-analytical pricing of a vast array of
contingent claims, including bonds, bond options, currency options, and credit default
swaps, via Fourier inversion techniques (Carr and Madan, 1999; Lewis, 2000).
8
Extreme Value Theory and Risk Measurement
The effective management of financial risk hinges critically on the accurate quantification of tail events—extreme market movements that, while rare, carry disproportionate
consequences for portfolio solvency, institutional stability, and systemic resilience. Traditional risk measures predicated on the normal distribution systematically underestimate
the frequency and severity of extreme losses, a deficiency tragically exposed during the
2008 global financial crisis (Danielsson, 2008). Extreme Value Theory (EVT) provides
a rigorous statistical framework for modeling the asymptotic behavior of maxima and
exceedances over high thresholds, offering principled extrapolation beyond the range of
observed data. This section develops the theoretical foundations of EVT, its application to Value-at-Risk (VaR) and Expected Shortfall (ES) estimation, the modeling of
dependence in extremes via copulas, and the measurement of systemic risk.
8.1
Value-at-Risk and Expected Shortfall: Definitions and Estimation
Risk measures condense the uncertainty of future portfolio losses into a single scalar
quantity. The choice of risk measure has profound implications for capital allocation,
performance evaluation, and regulatory compliance.
8.1.1
Value-at-Risk: Definition and Methodological Approaches
For a given confidence level α ∈(0, 1) (typically α = 0.95 or 0.99) and a holding period
∆t, the Value-at-Risk VaRα is the α-quantile of the loss distribution L = −(Pt+∆t −Pt):
VaRα = inf{ℓ∈R : P(L ≤ℓ) ≥α} = F −1
L (α).
(192)
Equivalently, VaRα is the maximum loss not exceeded with probability α. Despite its
intuitive appeal and widespread adoption under the Basel Accords, VaR suffers from
critical theoretical and practical limitations: it is not a coherent risk measure (violating subadditivity), ignores the severity of losses beyond the quantile, and is difficult to
estimate accurately in the far tail.
Table 5 summarizes the principal methodological approaches to VaR estimation, each
with distinct assumptions and limitations.

8.1.2
Expected Shortfall: Coherence and Elicitability
Expected Shortfall (ES), also termed Conditional Value-at-Risk (CVaR) or Tail Valueat-Risk (TVaR), addresses VaR’s insensitivity to the magnitude of tail losses.
For a
continuous loss distribution, ES is defined as the expected loss conditional on exceeding
VaR:
ESα = E[L|L > VaRα] =
1
1 −α
Z 1
α VaRu du.
(194)
78

Acerbi and Tasche (2002a) and Artzner and Heath (1999) established that ES satisfies
the axioms of a coherent risk measure: monotonicity, subadditivity, positive homogeneity, and translation invariance. Subadditivity (ρ(X + Y ) ≤ρ(X) + ρ(Y )) ensures
that diversification never increases measured risk—a property violated by VaR for nonelliptical distributions. Furthermore, Ziegel (2016) clarified the elicitability properties of
ES: while ES itself is not elicitable (cannot be uniquely minimized by a scoring function),
it is jointly elicitable with VaR, enabling consistent backtesting.
Reflecting these theoretical advantages, the Basel Committee on Banking Supervision
replaced VaR with ES at the 97.5% confidence level for market risk capital requirements
under the Fundamental Review of the Trading Book (FRTB) (Basel Committee on Banking Supervision, 2019). ES is estimated by averaging losses beyond the VaR threshold:

8.2
Extreme Value Theory:
Asymptotic Models for Maxima
and Exceedances
Extreme Value Theory provides the limit laws for properly normalized maxima and exceedances over high thresholds, enabling principled extrapolation into the tails of the
distribution.
8.2.1
The Fisher-Tippett-Gnedenko Theorem and Block Maxima
Let X1, X2, . . . , Xn be i.i.d. random variables with distribution function F, and let Mn =
max(X1, . . . , Xn) be the block maximum (e.g., annual maximum loss).
If there exist
normalizing sequences an > 0 and bn ∈R such that (Mn−bn)/an converges in distribution
to a non-degenerate limit G(z), then G belongs to the Generalized Extreme Value
(GEV) family (Jenkinson, 1955; Coles, 2001):
G(z; µ, σ, ξ) = exp
(
−

1 + ξ
z −µ
σ
−1/ξ)
,
{z : 1 + ξ(z −µ)/σ > 0},
(196)
where µ ∈R is the location parameter, σ > 0 is the scale parameter, and ξ ∈R is the
shape parameter governing tail heaviness.
The GEV family encompasses three sub-classes, distinguished by the sign of ξ:
• Fréchet (ξ > 0): Heavy-tailed distributions with polynomially decaying tails
(F(x) ∼x−1/ξ). The moments of order k ≥1/ξ are infinite. Financial loss data
typically belong to the Fréchet domain of attraction, consistent with the stylized
fact of heavy tails (McNeil and Embrechts, 2005).
• Gumbel (ξ = 0): Exponentially decaying tails (ξ →0 limit). Includes normal,
log-normal, and exponential distributions. The Gumbel domain is thin-tailed and
generally inadequate for financial extremes.
• Weibull (ξ < 0): Bounded upper tail. Rarely relevant for financial losses, which
are theoretically unbounded.
79

Block Maxima Method
In practice, the sample is divided into m non-overlapping
blocks of size n (e.g., n = 252 trading days for annual maxima). The GEV distribution is
fitted to the block maxima {Mn,1, . . . , Mn,m} via maximum likelihood. The 1−p quantile
(VaR) is then:
VaR1−p = µ −σ
ξ
h
1 −(−log(1 −p))−ξi
,
ξ̸ = 0.
(197)
The Block Maxima approach is intuitive but discards data by considering only one observation per block, leading to inefficient use of information.
8.2.2
Peaks-Over-Threshold and the Generalized Pareto Distribution
The Peaks-Over-Threshold (POT) method (Pickands, 1975; Davison, 1990) overcomes
the inefficiency of block maxima by modeling all exceedances above a sufficiently high
threshold u. The Pickands-Balkema-de Haan Theorem establishes that for a distribution F in the maximum domain of attraction of a GEV distribution, the distribution
of exceedances Y = X −u given X > u converges to the Generalized Pareto Distribution (GPD) as u →xF (the right endpoint):
H(y; σu, ξ) = 1 −

1 + ξ y
σu
−1/ξ
,
y ≥0,
(198)
defined on {y : y > 0, 1 + ξy/σu > 0}. Here, σu > 0 is the threshold-dependent scale
parameter and ξ is the identical shape parameter as in the GEV limit. The GPD is:
• Exponential (ξ = 0): H(y) = 1 −exp(−y/σu).
• Pareto (ξ > 0): Heavy-tailed, infinite moments for k ≥1/ξ.
• Beta (ξ < 0): Bounded support.
Threshold Selection
The choice of threshold u involves a fundamental bias-variance
trade-off:
• Too low u: Violates the asymptotic GPD approximation, introducing bias.
• Too high u: Reduces the number of exceedances, inflating parameter variance.
Diagnostic tools include:
• Mean Excess Plot: Plots the empirical mean excess e(u) = E[X −u|X > u]
against u. Linearity above a certain level indicates GPD validity.
• Parameter Stability Plots: Examines stability of ˆξ and ˆσu across a range of
candidate thresholds.
• Goodness-of-Fit Tests: Anderson-Darling and Cramér-von Mises tests applied
to the fitted GPD.
Scarrott and MacDonald (2012) provide a comprehensive review of threshold selection
methods.
80

Tail Probability and VaR Estimation via POT
Given a threshold u and exceedances Y1, . . . , YNu, the GPD parameters (σu, ξ) are estimated via maximum likelihood. The unconditional tail probability for x > u is:
P(X > x) = P(X > u) · P(X > x|X > u) ≈Nu

The Hill Estimator for Heavy Tails
For distributions in the Fréchet domain (ξ > 0),
the tail index α = 1/ξ governs the rate of tail decay.
Hill (1975) proposed a semiparametric estimator of α based on the k largest order statistics X(n−k+1) ≤· · · ≤X(n):
ˆα−1 = ˆξHill = 1
k
k
X

(202)
The Hill estimator is consistent and asymptotically normal under suitable conditions on
k (k →∞, k/n →0). The choice of k is critical and typically guided by Hill plots,
which graph ˆξHill against k, seeking a region of stability. Danielsson and de Vries (2001)
and Gabaix (2009) apply Hill-type estimators to characterize tail exponents in financial
and economic data.
8.2.3
Conditional Extreme Value Theory
Financial time series exhibit volatility clustering and time-varying tail heaviness, violating
the i.i.d. assumption of classical EVT. Conditional EVT integrates EVT with dynamic
models for the conditional mean and variance.
Two-Stage Conditional EVT (McNeil, 2000):
1. Fit a GARCH-type model to the return series: rt = µt + σtzt, where σt captures
conditional heteroskedasticity.
2. Extract standardized residuals ˆzt = (rt −ˆµt)/ˆσt.
3. Apply POT to the i.i.d. (or approximately i.i.d.) series {ˆzt} to estimate the tail of
the innovation distribution.
81

Conditional quantile forecasts are then obtained by rescaling:
d
VaRt+1(α) = ˆµt+1 +
ˆσt+1 d
VaRz(α), where
d
VaRz(α) is the POT VaR of the innovations. This approach yields
dynamic, responsive risk forecasts that adapt to changing market conditions.
Regression Models for EVT Parameters: Recent advances allow GPD or GEV
parameters to depend on covariates (Tang et al., 2026):
ξ(xt) = γTxt,
σ(xt) = exp(βTxt).
(203)
Covariates may include the VIX volatility index, credit spreads, or macroeconomic indicators, enabling the tail risk to vary systematically with the economic environment.
Estimation is typically via maximum likelihood or Bayesian MCMC.
8.3
Copulas and Multivariate Extreme Dependence
While univariate EVT quantifies the magnitude of individual tail losses, portfolio and
systemic risk hinge on the dependence among extreme events. Copulas provide a flexible
framework for modeling multivariate distributions by decoupling marginal behavior from
the dependence structure.
8.3.1
Sklar’s Theorem and Copula Fundamentals
Sklar (1959) established the foundational result: For any d-dimensional joint distribution
function F with continuous margins F1, . . . , Fd, there exists a unique copula C : [0, 1]d →
[0, 1] such that:
F(x1, . . . , xd) = C(F1(x1), . . . , Fd(xd)).
(204)
The copula C is a multivariate distribution function with uniform margins on [0, 1], completely characterizing the dependence among X1, . . . , Xd independently of their marginal
distributions. This separation property is invaluable for risk aggregation: margins can be
modeled with heavy-tailed distributions (e.g., GPD), while dependence is captured by a
copula with appropriate tail characteristics.
8.3.2
Canonical Copula Families and Tail Dependence
Elliptical Copulas
• Gaussian Copula: CGauss
R
(u1, . . . , ud) = ΦR(Φ−1(u1), . . . , Φ−1(ud)), where ΦR is
the joint normal CDF with correlation matrix R. The Gaussian copula exhibits
zero tail dependence: λL = λU = 0. Extreme events are asymptotically independent, a property catastrophically misaligned with empirical evidence of joint market
crashes. Embrechts (2002) and Salmon (2009) highlight the Gaussian copula’s role
in underestimating systemic risk prior to 2008.
• Student’s t Copula: Ct
ν,R(u1, . . . , ud) = tν,R(t−1

82

Archimedean Copulas
Archimedean copulas are generated by a convex, decreasing
generator function φ : [0, 1] →[0, ∞] with φ(1) = 0:
C(u1, . . . , ud) = φ−1(φ(u1) + · · · + φ(ud)).
(206)
Key families include:
• Clayton Copula: φ(t) = t−θ −1, θ > 0. Captures lower tail dependence
(λL = 2−1/θ, λU = 0), suitable for modeling joint downside risk.
• Gumbel Copula: φ(t) = (−log t)θ, θ ≥1. Captures upper tail dependence
(λL = 0, λU = 2 −21/θ).
• Frank Copula: No tail dependence; appropriate for symmetric dependence without extremes.
Extreme Value Copulas
Copulas that arise as limits of multivariate maxima are
extreme value copulas, satisfying C(ut
1, . . . , ut
d) = Ct(u1, . . . , ud) for t > 0.
The
Gumbel (1960) logistic model, Hüsler-Reiss, and Galambos copulas belong to this class
and provide asymptotically justified models for joint extremes (Gudendorf and Segers,
2010).
8.3.3

The lower and upper tail dependence coefficients quantify the probability of concurrent
extreme events:

demonstrates that tail dependence among international equity markets increases during financial crises, underscoring the inadequacy of static, crisis-insensitive dependence
models.
Regular Variation and Multivariate EVT
Multivariate extreme value theory characterizes the joint distribution of vector-valued extremes. A random vector X is multivariate regularly varying with tail index α > 0 if there exists a sequence bn →∞
such that:

systemic risk measures (Embrechts and Mikosch, 1997).
83

8.4
Systemic Risk Measurement
Systemic risk denotes the risk of widespread disruption to the financial system, wherein
distress in one institution propagates through interconnected balance sheets, fire sales,
and information contagion to impair the functioning of the broader economy (Bisias and
Valavanis, 2012). The 2008 crisis galvanized the development of quantitative systemic
risk measures.
Conditional Value-at-Risk (CoVaR)
Adrian and Brunnermeier (2016) propose CoVaRj|i
q
as the VaR of the financial system (or institution j) conditional on institution i being in
distress (i.e., its return equals its VaRi
q):
P

Rj ≤CoVaRj|i
q | Ri = VaRi
q

= q.
(210)
The systemic risk contribution of i is measured by ∆CoVaRj|i
q
= CoVaRj|i
q −VaRj
q, the
increase in system VaR attributable to i’s distress. CoVaR is typically estimated via
quantile regression (Adrian et al., 2019):
ˆRj = ˆα + ˆβRi + ˆγM,
(211)
where M are lagged state variables, yielding CoVaRj|i
